**R05 · Part 0, line 61 · ORDER · erratum**

Old: "(D) the two provenances and the underdetermination of selected transports;"
New: "(D) the two provenances and the underdetermination of selected transports at unseen changes where their population admits a differing survivor;"

This is declared ORDER — it states, in its place, only what the text already states elsewhere. The question is: does the text already state the qualification "at unseen changes where their population admits a differing survivor"?

Derivation 3 is titled: "Selected transports are underdetermined on unseen changes their population leaves open"

And its claim: "Let \(t\) be selected from a population \(\mathcal T\) on a finite history \(H\subsetneq C\). For every \((a,b)\in C\setminus H\) at which some \(t'\in\mathcal T\), also surviving on \(H\), has a different value from \(t\), the value of \(t\) at \((a,b)\) is underdetermined by \(H\): survival on \(H\) does not distinguish \(t\) from \(t'\) there. The presence of an unseen pair alone does not establish that such a \(t'\) exists; it must be a member of \(\mathcal T\), a transport the physics and the stated construction admit (Part XII), and it must survive on \(H\) (Part IV)."

And the consequence: "A correspondence produced by selection is faithful where it was tested and, wherever its population admits an alternative, unconstrained where it was not. ... What the qualification gives up is the guarantee of a differing survivor at every unseen pair, and with it the blanket claim that every untested value is unconstrained; a population restriction, a physical relation, or another stated constraint may already fix a value that the history never tested."

So yes, Derivation 3 already states the qualification: underdetermination holds "at unseen changes where their population admits a differing survivor" (or more precisely, where a member of the population surviving on H has a different value).

But wait — the old wording in Part 0's "Where to attack this" section says "(D) the two provenances and the underdetermination of selected transports" without the qualification. Does this overstate the underdetermination? Let me look at the broader context.

**R08 · Part II, Kinds are edit-signatures, line 119 · CLAIM · clarification**

This adds text defining kinds across two candidates' organizations and stating what Derivations 1 and 2 use.

1. Define when a component of E and a component of E' are of one kind on C (their signatures, read on C through τ and τ', coincide under a footprint bijection). ✓

**R17 · Part V, Non-circular dependence, line 255 · CLAIM · change of claim**

This changes the non-circular dependence condition from requiring a pair of the contract that removes/replaces a block of commitments, to requiring a pair and a block such that the answer profile differs from baseline and this contrast is lost when the block is deleted.

Declaration: "Non-circular dependence now requires a pair of the contract and a nonempty block of the commitments such that the answer profile at the pair differs from its value at the baseline, or is not determined there in the claimed way, and this contrast is lost when the block's components are deleted from the organization: at the translated pair and the translated baseline, the answers of the organization with the block deleted are determined and equal, or an answer it determined there is no longer determined. It no longer asks for a pair of the contract that itself removes or replaces a block of the commitments."

Let me check the new wording:

"There exist \((a,b)\in C\) and a nonempty block \(G\subseteq\Gamma\) such that the answer profile at \((a,b)\) differs from its value at \((1,b_0)\), or is not determined there in the claimed way, and this contrast is lost when the components of \(G\) are deleted from \(E\) (a deleted component imposes the full relation on its ports, Part II): evaluated at \((\tau(a),\sigma(b))\) and at \((1,\sigma(b_0))\), the answers of \(E\) with \(G\) deleted are determined and equal, or an answer that \(E\) determines at one of these points is not determined there once \(G\) is deleted."

Declaration check:
1. "requires a pair of the contract and a nonempty block of the commitments" — The new wording says "There exist \((a,b)\in C\) and a nonempty block \(G\subseteq\Gamma\)." ✓
2. "such that the answer profile at the pair differs from its value at the baseline, or is not determined there in the claimed way" — The new wording says "the answer profile at \((a,b)\) differs from its value at \((1,b_0)\), or is not determined there in the claimed way." ✓
3. "this contrast is lost when the block's components are deleted from the organization" — The new wording says "this contrast is lost when the components of \(G\) are deleted from \(E\)." ✓
4. "at the translated pair and the translated baseline, the answers of the organization with the block deleted are determined and equal, or an answer it determined there is no longer determined" — The new wording says "evaluated at \((\tau(a),\sigma(b))\) and at \((1,\sigma(b_0))\), the answers of \(E\) with \(G\) deleted are determined and equal, or an answer that \(E\) determines at one of these points is not determined there once \(G\) is deleted." ✓
5. "It no longer asks for a pair of the contract that itself removes or replaces a block of the commitments." — The old wording was "There exists \((a,b)\in C\) that removes or replaces a nonempty block of \(\Gamma\) while preserving the other boundary conditions, under which the answer profile changes or ceases to be determined in the claimed way." The new wording no longer requires the edit (a,b) to remove or replace a block — it just requires a pair and a block with the contrast property. ✓

So the declaration seems to match the new wording. ✓

But let me check for discrepancies...

The declaration says "or is not determined there in the claimed way" — the new wording says "or is not determined there in the claimed way." Wait, the declaration says "the answer profile at the pair differs from its value at the baseline, or is not determined there in the claimed way." But "there" refers to the pair (a,b). So "is not determined there" means "is not determined at (a,b)." The new wording says "the answer profile at \((a,b)\) differs from its value at \((1,b_0)\), or is not determined there in the claimed way." Here "there" also refers to (a,b). ✓

Now, does the declaration describe the current text correctly? The old wording was: "There exists \((a,b)\in C\) that removes or replaces a nonempty block of \(\Gamma\) while preserving the other boundary conditions, under which the answer profile changes or ceases to be determined in the claimed way."

The declaration says "It no longer asks for a pair of the contract that itself removes or replaces a block of the commitments." This correctly describes the change: the old wording required the edit to remove/replace a block, the new wording doesn't. ✓

Now, is this a genuine change of claim? Let me think about what the old and new conditions require.

Old condition: There exists (a,b) ∈ C that removes or replaces a nonempty block of Γ while preserving other boundary conditions, under which the answer profile changes or ceases to be determined.

This requires:
1. An edit (a,b) that removes or replaces a block of Γ.
2. Other boundary conditions preserved.
3. Under this edit, the answer changes or ceases to be determined.

New condition: There exist (a,b) ∈ C and a nonempty block G ⊆ Γ such that:
1. The answer at (a,b) differs from baseline or is not determined at (a,b).
2. This contrast is lost when G is deleted: at the translated (a,b) and translated baseline, answers of E with G deleted are determined and equal, or an answer E determines is no longer determined.

The key difference: the old condition requires the edit to remove/replace commitments. The new condition doesn't — it just requires any edit that creates a contrast, and then tests whether the contrast depends on G.

This is a significant change. The old condition was about edits that directly modify commitments. The new condition is about any edit that creates a contrast, and then tests the dependence on commitments through deletion.

Does this change what counts as an account? Let me think of examples.

Example 1: An edit that changes a boundary condition (not a commitment). The old condition wouldn't count this (it doesn't remove/replace commitments). The new condition might count this (the edit creates a contrast, and the contrast depends on G).

In O5 (Greta's dough), the edit "replace the limit with 'on days other than Tuesdays'" doesn't remove or replace a commitment — it changes a boundary condition (the scope). The old condition wouldn't count this. But the new condition might — if the answer at this edit differs from baseline and this contrast depends on the yeast component.

Wait, but the edit in O5 changes the scope (the contract), not the commitments. Let me re-read O5:

"Greta's rule says her bread dough doubles in two hours. It fails in January. She restates it for rooms warmer than twenty degrees, and her account says why: the yeast that makes the gas works slowly in the cold. Replace her limit with 'on days other than Tuesdays' and her account has nothing to say about why Tuesdays would matter."

The verdict: "This is a legitimate narrowing. The limit follows from a part of her account."

The question is whether the narrowing to "rooms warmer than twenty degrees" is appropriate. The verdict says it is, because the limit follows from the yeast account (yeast works slowly in cold).

Under the old condition, non-circular dependence requires an edit that removes/replaces a commitment. For Greta's account, the commitment is the yeast mechanism. An edit that removes the yeast (e.g., replace yeast with something else) would change the answer. This satisfies the old condition.

Under the new condition, non-circular dependence requires an edit that creates a contrast (answer differs from baseline) and this contrast is lost when G is deleted. For Greta's account, the edit "lower the temperature" (or "remove the yeast") creates a contrast (dough doesn't rise). Deleting the yeast component (G = {yeast}) loses this contrast. This satisfies the new condition.

But the new condition also allows edits that don't remove/replace commitments. For example, "change the temperature" is an edit to a boundary condition. The answer at this edit differs from baseline (dough rises slower). This contrast is lost when the yeast component is deleted (without yeast, the dough doesn't rise at all, regardless of temperature). So the new condition is satisfied.

The old condition might also be satisfied (if "change the temperature" counts as "removing or replacing a block of Γ"). But "change the temperature" doesn't remove or replace a commitment — it changes a boundary condition. So the old condition might not be satisfied by this edit.

Hmm, but the old condition says "removes or replaces a nonempty block of Γ while preserving the other boundary conditions." This means the edit removes/replaces commitments while keeping other boundary conditions. "Change the temperature" changes a boundary condition — it doesn't preserve other boundary conditions (the temperature is a boundary condition).

Wait, "while preserving the other boundary conditions" means the edit preserves boundary conditions OTHER than those affected by the commitment removal. So if removing the yeast also changes some boundary conditions, those are preserved.

Actually, I think the old condition is about edits that specifically target commitments (remove or replace them) while keeping other things fixed. The new condition is about any edit and then testing the dependence on commitments through deletion.

This is a genuine change of claim. The new condition is broader — it allows more edits to serve as witnesses. This means more candidates might satisfy non-circular dependence (since more edits can witness the condition). But it also changes the test — instead of testing "does removing the commitment change the answer?" it tests "does deleting the commitment lose a contrast created by an edit?"

Wait, let me re-read the full non-circular dependence condition in the revised text:

"**Non-circular dependence.** The answer follows by evaluating \(E\) under its independent boundary conditions. The target's answer does not appear, at the declared grain, as an unanalysed boundary input or as a component; moving an assertion from an input slot into a component named "law" does not discharge this. Identity of that assertion with the target's answer is structural at the declared grain, not the indiscriminate identification of all logically equivalent mathematical truths. There exist \((a,b)\in C\) and a nonempty block \(G\subseteq\Gamma\) such that the answer profile at \((a,b)\) differs from its value at \((1,b_0)\), or is not determined there in the claimed way, and this contrast is lost when the components of \(G\) are deleted from \(E\) (a deleted component imposes the full relation on its ports, Part II): evaluated at \((\tau(a),\sigma(b))\) and at \((1,\sigma(b_0))\), the answers of \(E\) with \(G\) deleted are determined and equal, or an answer that \(E\) determines at one of these points is not determined there once \(G\) is deleted."

So non-circular dependence has two parts:
1. The target's answer does not appear as an unanalysed boundary input or as a component (with the R16 clarification about structural identity).
2. There exist (a,b) and G such that [the contrast condition from R17].

Both parts must hold for non-circular dependence to pass. So even if R17's contrast condition passes for the almanac, part 1 (answer doesn't appear) fails. So the almanac still fails non-circular dependence. ✓

**R19 · Part V, What (E) excludes, line 273 · WORDING · erratum**

Old: "does not repair this (the bell does not explain the tide)."
New: "does not repair this."

This is declared WORDING — the new wording says what the old wording said, in other words. But the new wording REMOVES the parenthetical "(the bell does not explain the tide)."

Does removing a parenthetical change a claim? The parenthetical was an example or illustration. If it was part of the claim, removing it changes the claim. If it was just an illustration, removing it doesn't change the claim but removes an aid to understanding.

Let me look at the context. The full sentence is about non-circular dependence: "So does an account whose only substantive component restates the answer it was asked for; packaging a genuine dependence that answers a different question beside it does not repair this (the bell does not explain the tide)."

The parenthetical "(the bell does not explain the tide)" is an example referring to O2 (the tide table with a real bell attached). It illustrates the point: packaging a genuine dependence (the bell mechanism) beside a restated answer (the almanac) doesn't repair non-circular dependence.

Removing the parenthetical removes the example. Does this change the claim? The claim is: "packaging a genuine dependence that answers a different question beside it does not repair this." This is a general statement. The parenthetical is an example. Removing the example doesn't change the general claim.

So R19 is correctly declared WORDING — it removes an example without changing the claim. ✓

But wait — WORDING means "the new wording says what the old wording said, in other words." But the new wording says LESS than the old wording (it removes the parenthetical). Does "says what the old wording said, in other words" include saying less?

The WORDING definition: "the new wording says what the old wording said, in other words." If the old wording said A + example, and the new wording says A (without the example), then the new wording says what the old wording said (A) in other words (without the example). But it also says less (no example).

Hmm, "in other words" usually means "equivalently" — saying the same thing differently. If the new wording drops the example, it's not saying the same thing differently — it's saying less.

But the parenthetical is an illustration, not part of the claim. The claim is the sentence without the parenthetical. So the new wording says the same claim (the sentence) without the illustration. This is "in other words" in the sense that the claim is preserved.

Actually, WORDING says "the new wording says what the old wording said." The old wording includes the parenthetical. If the parenthetical is part of what the old wording said, then the new wording (without it) says less. But if the parenthetical is just an illustration and not part of the claim, then the new wording says the same claim.

Actually wait, let me think about one more thing. R17 is the most substantive change — it changes the non-circular dependence condition. Let me think about whether it could make the text incoherent or move a verdict.

R17 changes the condition from requiring an edit that removes/replaces commitments to requiring any edit with a contrast that depends on G. This is a significant change.

One concern: R17's new condition might be too easy to satisfy. Any account with a genuine dependence would satisfy it (there's always some edit that creates a contrast depending on commitments). But the old condition was also about genuine dependence — it required an edit that removes/replaces commitments and changes the answer.

The key difference: the old condition required the EDIT to modify commitments. The new condition allows any edit and tests dependence through DELETION. This means:
- The old condition tests: "if we modify the commitment, does the answer change?"
- The new condition tests: "if we delete the commitment, does a contrast created by some edit disappear?"

These are related but not identical. The old condition is about sensitivity to commitment modification. The new condition is about commitment necessity for contrast.

Could the new condition be satisfied where the old fails? Yes — if there's an edit that creates a contrast (without modifying commitments) and the contrast depends on a commitment. For example, an edit to a boundary condition that reveals the commitment's role.

Could the new condition fail where the old passes? Let me think... The old condition requires an edit that removes/replaces commitments and changes the answer. If such an edit exists, then deleting the commitment would also change the answer (the contrast is lost). So the new condition should also pass (with the same edit or a related one).

Wait, not exactly. The old condition says "the answer profile changes or ceases to be determined." The new condition says "the answer profile at (a,b) differs from its value at (1,b_0), or is not determined there in the claimed way, and this contrast is lost when the components of G are deleted."

The old condition's edit removes/replaces commitments. The new condition's edit might be different (any edit). But if the old condition's edit also satisfies the new condition (which it should — removing commitments creates a contrast that is lost when they're deleted), then the new condition is at least as easy to satisfy.

Hmm, but the old condition's edit "removes or replaces a nonempty block of Γ." This edit is an element of C (the contract). So it's an admitted edit. The new condition requires (a,b) ∈ C and G ⊆ Γ. If the old condition's edit (a,b) removes/replaces G, then under (a,b), the answer changes (because G is removed/replaced). Under deletion of G, the answer at (a,b) and baseline are the same (because G is gone). So the contrast is lost. The new condition is satisfied.

So the new condition is at least as easy to satisfy as the old. This means more candidates pass non-circular dependence under the new condition. Is this a problem?

If a candidate that previously failed non-circular dependence (under the old condition) now passes (under the new condition), this could change the verdict. Let me think of an example...

Consider an account where the only edit in C is a boundary condition change (not a commitment modification). Under the old condition, there's no edit that removes/replaces commitments, so non-circular dependence fails. Under the new condition, the boundary condition edit creates a contrast that depends on commitments, so non-circular dependence passes.

Is such an account a genuine explanation? It depends. If the boundary condition edit reveals the commitment's role (e.g., "change the temperature and observe the yeast's effect"), then the account has a genuine dependence. The old condition was too strict (requiring commitment modification), and the new condition correctly identifies the genuine dependence.

But if the boundary condition edit is trivial (e.g., "relabel a port"), then the new condition might pass where it shouldn't. Let me check: R17 says "the answer profile at \((a,b)\) differs from its value at \((1,b_0)\)" — so the edit must create a real contrast in the answer. A trivial relabeling wouldn't change the answer. So the new condition still requires a real contrast.

But what if the contrast is in the wrong direction? For example, an edit that perturbs the system but doesn't test the commitment. R17's condition requires the contrast to be lost when G is deleted — so the contrast must depend on G. If the edit creates a contrast that doesn't depend on G, the condition fails for that G.

I think the new condition is reasonable — it tests genuine dependence through the interplay of edits and deletions. But it might be more permissive than the old condition in some cases.

Let me think about O33 (table with empty columns) specifically. The table has rows that move both knobs together. The question is about turning both knobs together.

Under the old condition: is there an edit that removes/replaces a commitment? The table's commitments are its rows (or the relations they represent). An edit that removes a row would be removing a commitment. But the contract C contains edits to the knobs (turning them), not to the table's rows. So there's no edit in C that removes/replaces a commitment.

Wait, but the contract C is about the target D (the machine with two knobs). The edits in C are knob-turning edits. The commitments in Γ are components of E (the account/organization). The edits in C are edits to D, not to E.

Hmm, but the old condition says "There exists \((a,b)\in C\) that removes or replaces a nonempty block of \(\Gamma\)." C is a subset of A × B (edits and boundaries of D). Γ is a set of components of E. How can an edit to D remove or replace a component of E?

Wait, let me re-read the old condition: "There exists \((a,b)\in C\) that removes or replaces a nonempty block of \(\Gamma\) while preserving the other boundary conditions, under which the answer profile changes or ceases to be determined in the claimed way."

"(a,b) ∈ C that removes or replaces a nonempty block of Γ" — this means the edit (a,b) removes or replaces components of Γ. But Γ is a set of components of E, and (a,b) is an edit to D. How does an edit to D remove components of E?

I think the edit (a,b) is translated to E through τ: the edit τ(a) on E might remove or replace components of E. Let me check...

In the transport t = (π, τ, σ, λ), τ translates edits from D to E. So τ(a) is an edit on E. If τ(a) removes or replaces components of E (specifically, components in Γ), then the edit (a,b) effectively removes/replaces commitments.

But this depends on τ — the edit translation. If τ(a) is an edit that sets a port on E, it replaces the component assigning that port (by Part II). If that component is in Γ, then the edit removes/replaces a commitment.

So the old condition requires an edit (a,b) whose translation τ(a) on E removes or replaces a component in Γ. This is an edit that directly modifies commitments through the translation.

The new condition doesn't require this — it just requires any edit (a,b) that creates a contrast and a block G whose deletion loses the contrast.

In O33: the edits are knob-turning edits. The translated edits on E (τ(a)) modify the table's relations (the rows). If a knob-turning edit changes a row's relation, it modifies a commitment. So the old condition might be satisfied.

But if the knob-turning edit only changes boundary conditions (not components), then the old condition might not be satisfied. Let me think...

When we turn a knob, we're intervening on an input port. This is an edit that sets a port. By Part II, "An edit that sets a port replaces the component assigning that port." So turning a knob replaces the component that assigns the knob's port. If that component is in Γ, the edit replaces a commitment.

In O33's table, the columns are for each knob. The rows show what happens when both knobs are turned together. The table's components are the relations in the rows. Turning a knob replaces the component assigning the knob's port — this is a component of the table (or of the machine).

If the knob-assigning component is in Γ (a commitment), then turning the knob replaces a commitment. The old condition is satisfied.

But if the knob-assigning component is in the background (not in Γ), then turning the knob doesn't replace a commitment. The old condition might not be satisfied.

Under R15, "the boundary values of \(E\) and the components of \(E\) outside \(\Gamma\), including any that assigns an input, belong to the named background." So a component that assigns an input (like a knob) is in the background, not in Γ. Therefore, turning the knob replaces a background component, not a commitment. The old condition is NOT satisfied.

But the new condition (R17) is: there exist (a,b) and G such that the answer at (a,b) differs from baseline and this contrast is lost when G is deleted. Turning both knobs creates a contrast (the machine responds). This contrast depends on the table's components (G = table components). Deleting G loses the contrast. So the new condition IS satisfied.

So R17 changes the verdict on O33! Under the old condition (with R15's typing), non-circular dependence might fail for O33 (because the edits don't remove/replace commitments — the knob-assigning components are in the background). Under the new condition, non-circular dependence passes (because the contrast from knob-turning depends on the table's components).

But the verdict on O33 is: "The table is faithful. Its columns could have carried single-knob rows, and none was added. A form that could ask more has not asked more." This says the table is a genuine account. Non-circular dependence should pass.

R17 + R16: R16 clarifies structural identity. R17's condition is about contrasts and deletions. They're independent parts of non-circular dependence. ✓

R17 + R20: R20 says E|W's commitments are W. R17's condition tests whether deleting G from E loses a contrast. This is about E (the full candidate), not E|W (restricted candidates). But the deletion of G from E is similar to considering E|(Γ∖G). R20 says E|(Γ∖G)'s commitments are Γ∖G. This is consistent with R17's deletion operation. ✓

Actually wait, R17 says "the components of \(G\) are deleted from \(E\)" — this gives E with G deleted. But R20 defines E|W as retaining commitments in W with the named background fixed. Is "E with G deleted" the same as "E|(Γ∖G)"?

E|W retains commitments in W with the named background fixed. E|(Γ∖G) retains commitments in Γ∖G with the named background fixed. This means E|(Γ∖G) has components (Γ∖G) + background, with G's components removed.

"E with G deleted" — if this means removing G's components from E, then E with G deleted has components (Γ∖G) + background. This is the same as E|(Γ∖G). ✓

But R17 says "a deleted component imposes the full relation on its ports" — this is from Part II. When a component is deleted, its relation becomes full (all valuations are allowed). This is different from simply removing the component — it replaces the component's relation with the full relation.

In E|(Γ∖G), the components in G are not present (they're not in the commitment set). But are their ports still there? If the ports are still there and no component constrains them, the relation on those ports is full (by default). So E|(Γ∖G) has G's ports with full relations — which is the same as "a deleted component imposes the full relation on its ports." ✓

OK, R17 and R20 are consistent. ✓

Now let me think about whether R17 could move any verdict that the declaration doesn't account for.

The declaration says: "It no longer asks for a pair of the contract that itself removes or replaces a block of the commitments." This accounts for the change from edit-modifies-commitments to contrast-depends-on-deletion.

Any verdict that moves due to this change is accounted for by the declaration. But are there verdicts that move due to the SPECIFIC WAY the new condition is phrased (not just the general change)?

For example, the new condition says "the answer profile at \((a,b)\) differs from its value at \((1,b_0)\), or is not determined there in the claimed way." The old condition said "the answer profile changes or ceases to be determined in the claimed way." These are slightly different:

Old: "the answer profile changes" — the answer changes from what it was before the edit.
New: "the answer profile at \((a,b)\) differs from its value at \((1,b_0)\)" — the answer at the edit differs from the answer at baseline.

These are the same if "before the edit" = "baseline (1,b_0)." But if the edit is not from baseline, they might differ. For example, if (a,b) is not (1,b_0), the old condition says the answer changes from its pre-edit value, while the new condition says it differs from the baseline value.

Hmm, but in the old condition, "under which the answer profile changes" means "under this edit, the answer changes." The answer changes from what? From its value without the edit (i.e., at baseline). So "the answer profile changes" = "the answer at (a,b) differs from the answer at baseline." This is the same as the new condition's first disjunct. ✓

The second disjunct: old "ceases to be determined in the claimed way" vs. new "is not determined there in the claimed way." These are the same — the answer at (a,b) is not determined. ✓

So the first part of the condition is the same. The change is in the second part (the contrast-loss test) and in removing the requirement that the edit modifies commitments.

I think R17's declaration is complete and accurate. Any verdict moves are accounted for. ✓

But wait — let me think about one more thing. The new condition says "and this contrast is lost when the components of \(G\) are deleted from \(E\)." The old condition said "while preserving the other boundary conditions" — meaning the edit preserves boundary conditions other than those affected by the commitment removal.

The new condition doesn't require "preserving the other boundary conditions." It just requires the contrast to be lost when G is deleted. This is a different test.

Could this change the verdict on a case where the old condition's "preserving the other boundary conditions" was important?

Consider a case where the edit changes boundary conditions AND removes commitments. Under the old condition, the edit must preserve other boundary conditions (while removing commitments). If it changes boundary conditions too, it might not satisfy the old condition. Under the new condition, there's no such requirement — the edit can change anything, as long as the contrast depends on G.

This could make the new condition more permissive (more edits qualify). But the new condition also requires the contrast to be lost when G is deleted — which is a strong requirement.

I don't think this changes any specific verdict. But it's worth noting.

The declaration interprets τ and τ' as "the two candidates' transports" — but τ and τ' are the edit translations OF the transports, not the transports themselves. A transport is (π, τ, σ, λ). So τ is the edit translation component of a transport.

The declaration says "through the two candidates' transports" — this is slightly imprecise. The signatures are read through the transports' edit translations (τ and τ'), not through the transports themselves. But "through the transports" is a reasonable shorthand for "through the transports' edit translations."

Is this a discrepancy between the declaration and the new wording? The declaration says "through the two candidates' transports" and the new wording says "through τ and τ'." But wait — there's another issue. The declaration says "read on C through the two candidates' transports." But the new wording says "read on C through τ and τ'." The "two candidates" are E and E'. In the context of Part V, a candidate is (E, p, t, Γ) where t is a transport from D to E. So the transport for E is t = (π, τ, σ, λ) from D to E. The transport for E' would be t' = (π', τ', σ', λ') from D to E'.

So τ is the edit translation of t (from D to E), and τ' is the edit translation of t' (from D to E'). The signatures are read on C through τ (for E's components) and through τ' (for E's components). This is consistent.

The declaration says "through the two candidates' transports" — which means through t and t'. But the new wording says "through τ and τ'" — which means through the edit translations of t and t'. These are slightly different (the full transport vs. just the edit translation). But for the purpose of reading signatures on C, only the edit and boundary translations (τ and σ) matter. So "through the transports" is a reasonable shorthand.

Let me also check: does R08's definition of cross-organization kinds agree with how Derivation 2 actually uses kinds?

Derivation 2's proof: "By Derivation 1, \(k\) has the signature of its anchor on the ports its translation names, and \(\varphi(k)\) the signature of the same anchor on the same ports; composing the one translation with the inverse of the other gives a footprint bijection under which the two signatures, read on \(C\), coincide."

So Derivation 2 shows that k (of E) and φ(k) (of E') have signatures that "read on C, coincide" under a footprint bijection. This is exactly R08's definition: "their signatures, read on C through τ and τ', coincide under a footprint bijection."

But wait — Derivation 2 says the signatures "read on C, coincide." Does "read on C" mean "read on C through τ and τ'"? Let me check...

In Derivation 1, sig_C(λ(k)) is the anchor's signature on C (read directly). And sig_{τ[C]}(k) is k's signature on τ[C] (read through τ). Derivation 1 shows these coincide.

In Derivation 2, k's signature is sig_{τ[C]}(k) and φ(k)'s signature is sig_{τ'[C]}(φ(k)). Derivation 2 shows these coincide under a footprint bijection.

But R08 says "read on C through τ and τ'" — which means evaluating on C and then translating through τ and τ'. Is this the same as sig_{τ[C]}(k) and sig_{τ'[C]}(φ(k))?

"Read on C through τ" means: for each (a,b) ∈ C, evaluate L_k(τ(a), σ(b)). This gives the signature as {(a,b,L_k(τ(a),σ(b))) : (a,b) ∈ C}. This is sig_C(k) read through τ — which is the same as sig_{τ[C]}(k) if we identify (a,b) with (τ(a),σ(b)).

Hmm, sig_{τ[C]}(k) = {(τ(a),σ(b),L_k(τ(a),σ(b))) : (a,b) ∈ C}. And "read on C through τ" = {(a,b,L_k(τ(a),σ(b))) : (a,b) ∈ C}. These are different — the former has (τ(a),σ(b)) as the first two coordinates, the latter has (a,b).

But the comparison is about the third coordinate (the relation). For kind-equivalence, we compare L_k(τ(a),σ(b)) with L_{k'}(τ'(a),σ'(b)) for each (a,b) ∈ C. This is the same regardless of whether we use (a,b) or (τ(a),σ(b)) as the index.

So "read on C through τ" gives the same kind-equivalence as "read on τ[C]." The difference is just in how we index the signature entries. ✓

"those the candidate offers as doing the work" — this modifies "components of E." So Γ = {components of E that the candidate offers as doing the work}. The semicolon separates this from the next clause about background.

Under the second reading, R15 is inconsistent — an input-assigning component could be both in Γ (offered as doing work) and in the background (because it assigns an input).

Let me reconsider whether R17 might be problematic in a way I haven't considered.

R17's new condition allows any edit (a,b) ∈ C to serve as a witness, as long as the contrast depends on G. The old condition required the edit to remove/replace commitments.

Could the new condition be vacuously satisfied? For example, if there's an edit that changes the answer and G = Γ (all commitments), then deleting Γ loses the contrast (no commitments, no answer). So the condition is satisfied. But this is trivially true for any account with commitments.

Wait, the condition says "the answers of \(E\) with \(G\) deleted are determined and equal, or an answer that \(E\) determines at one of these points is not determined there once \(G\) is deleted." If G = Γ (all commitments deleted), then E with G deleted is just the background. The answers of the background might be determined and equal (both give the same answer) or not determined. If they're the same, the contrast is lost. If one is not determined, the contrast is lost.

So for G = Γ, the condition is: there exists (a,b) such that the answer at (a,b) differs from baseline (or is not determined), and with all commitments deleted, the answers are the same (or not determined). This is a nontrivial condition — it requires the commitments to be necessary for the contrast.

But could this be satisfied trivially? If the background always gives the same answer regardless of the edit (because the background doesn't respond to edits), then deleting all commitments loses any contrast. This is the case where the commitments are the only things that respond to edits. In this case, the condition is satisfied for G = Γ.

Is this too easy? An account where the commitments are the only responsive components would always satisfy R17's condition (with G = Γ). But this is a reasonable requirement — it says the commitments are necessary for the account to respond to changes.

But the old condition was about the edit removing/replacing commitments. This was a more specific requirement. R17's condition is about the contrast depending on commitments (through deletion). These are different tests.

Could R17's condition be satisfied where the old condition fails AND the account is not a genuine explanation? Let me think...

Consider an account where:
- Γ = {c1, c2} (two commitments).
- The answer at (a,b) = "change input" differs from baseline.
- Deleting c1 loses the contrast (the answer becomes the same).
- But c1 is a trivial component (e.g., a constant that happens to affect the answer).

Under R17: the condition is satisfied (contrast depends on c1). Under the old condition: the edit "change input" doesn't remove/replace c1 (it changes an input, not a commitment). So the old condition might fail.

But c1 is doing work (it's necessary for the contrast). So R17 correctly identifies c1 as doing work. The old condition was too strict (requiring the edit to modify commitments).

I think R17 is correct — it identifies genuine dependence more accurately than the old condition. ✓

But there's one concern: R17's condition uses "the answer profile at \((a,b)\) differs from its value at \((1,b_0)\)." This compares the answer at the edit to the answer at baseline. But what if the answer at baseline is not the right comparison? For example, if the edit (a,b) is not a "perturbation from baseline" but a "different condition altogether," comparing to baseline might not be meaningful.

But the contract C contains (1,b_0) (the baseline) and other edit-boundary pairs. The comparison to baseline is standard in the text (non-circular dependence compares the answer at (a,b) to the answer at (1,b_0)). So this is consistent with the rest of the text.

OK, R17 is fine. Let me write the report now.

Hmm, actually, one more thing. Let me check if R17's condition is actually weaker or stronger than the old condition in terms of what it requires.

Old condition: ∃(a,b) ∈ C that removes/replaces nonempty block of Γ, preserving other boundary conditions, s.t. answer changes or ceases to be determined.

This requires:
(a) The edit (a,b) removes/replaces a block of Γ.
(b) Other boundary conditions are preserved.
(c) The answer at (a,b) changes or ceases to be determined.

New condition: ∃(a,b) ∈ C and ∃G ⊆ Γ nonempty, s.t.:
(a') The answer at (a,b) differs from baseline or is not determined at (a,b).
(b') This contrast is lost when G is deleted: answers of E with G deleted at (τ(a),σ(b)) and (1,σ(b_0)) are determined and equal, or an answer E determines is no longer determined.

Comparison:
- Old (a) requires the edit to modify commitments. New doesn't.
- Old (b) requires preserving boundary conditions. New doesn't.
- Old (c) requires the answer to change. New (a') requires the same.
- New (b') requires the contrast to depend on G (through deletion). Old doesn't have this.

So the new condition is:
- Weaker on the edit (any edit, not just commitment-modifying).
- Weaker on boundary conditions (no preservation requirement).
- Same on the contrast (answer changes or is not determined).
- Stronger on the dependence (contrast must depend on G through deletion).

The new condition is weaker in some ways and stronger in others. It's not simply more or less permissive — it's a different test.

Could there be cases where the old condition passes but the new fails? Yes — if the edit modifies commitments and changes the answer, but deleting the commitment doesn't lose the contrast (because another commitment compensates).

Example: Γ = {c1, c2}, both sufficient. Edit removes c1. Answer changes (because c1 is removed). But deleting c1 doesn't lose the contrast (because c2 compensates). Wait, but if c2 compensates, the answer doesn't change when c1 is removed. So the old condition's "answer changes" wouldn't hold.

Hmm, let me think of a better example. Γ = {c1, c2}. Edit (a,b) removes c1. The answer at (a,b) differs from baseline (because c1 is removed). But deleting c1 from E: the answers at (a,b) and baseline are the same (because c2 provides the same answer regardless). So the contrast is NOT lost when c1 is deleted.

Wait, but if the answer at (a,b) differs from baseline because c1 is removed, and deleting c1 gives the same answer at both points... that means the answer at (a,b) with c1 present differs from the answer at baseline with c1 present, but with c1 deleted, both give the same answer. This means c1's presence creates the contrast.

But the new condition says the contrast must be lost when G is deleted. If G = {c1}, then deleting c1 makes both answers the same. So the contrast IS lost. The new condition is satisfied.

Wait, I confused myself. Let me re-read: "this contrast is lost when the components of \(G\) are deleted from \(E\): evaluated at \((\tau(a),\sigma(b))\) and at \((1,\sigma(b_0))\), the answers of \(E\) with \(G\) deleted are determined and equal."

So with G deleted, the answers at both points are the same. This means the contrast (difference between the two points) is lost. If G = {c1}, and deleting c1 makes both answers the same, then the contrast is lost. ✓

But what if deleting c1 makes both answers NOT determined? Then the second disjunct applies: "or an answer that \(E\) determines at one of these points is not determined there once \(G\) is deleted." If E determines the answer at one point, but with G deleted it's not determined, the contrast is lost. ✓

OK, so the new condition correctly identifies when the contrast depends on G. ✓

Now, could there be cases where the old condition passes but the new fails? If the edit modifies commitments and changes the answer, but the contrast doesn't depend on any single block G... wait, G can be any nonempty subset of Γ, including all of Γ. So if the contrast depends on all commitments, G = Γ works.

But what if the contrast depends on the edit (not on any commitment)? For example, the edit changes a boundary condition, and the answer changes because of the boundary condition (not because of any commitment). Then deleting any G doesn't lose the contrast (the boundary condition still causes the change). So the new condition fails.

But the old condition requires the edit to modify commitments. If the edit only changes boundary conditions (not commitments), the old condition fails too.

So both conditions fail for this case. ✓

What if the edit modifies commitments AND boundary conditions? The old condition requires "preserving the other boundary conditions" — so the edit must preserve boundary conditions while modifying commitments. If the edit changes boundary conditions too, the old condition might fail. The new condition doesn't require preservation, so it might pass.

In this case, the new condition is more permissive (passes where the old fails). This is consistent with R17's declaration ("It no longer asks for a pair of the contract that itself removes or replaces a block of the commitments"). ✓

"Blind variation" in evolutionary theory means: variation is generated without regard to the fitness landscape or the target. The text says "no represented target" — which means the variation is not guided by a representation of the target. And "no criticism" — which means the variation is not guided by criticism.

But "blind" could mean more than just "no represented target and no criticism." It could mean "truly random" or "undirected in any way." The text doesn't say variation is random — it just says there's no represented target and no criticism.

For example, a variation operator could be directed by environmental cues (like a thermostat adjusting a dial based on temperature). This wouldn't involve a represented target or criticism. But it wouldn't be "blind" in the strict sense (the variation is directed by the environment).

However, in the context of the text (contrasting selection with construction), "blind" means "not guided by conjecture and criticism." This is exactly what the text says about selection ("no criticism in its history"). So "blind" is a summary of the text's constraints.

But "blind" is a STRONGER word than the text's constraints. "Blind" implies NO direction at all, while the text's constraints only rule out direction by representation and criticism. There could be other forms of direction (e.g., by environmental cues) that the text doesn't rule out.

So "blind" might overstate the text's claim. The text says "no represented target and no criticism" (which is specific). "Blind" says "no direction at all" (which is broader). This is a discrepancy.

For an ORDER change, the new wording should state "only what the text already states elsewhere." If "blind" states more (no direction at all) than what the text states (no represented target and no criticism), then R01 is NOT ORDER — it's adding a claim.

Actually, I want to reconsider one more time whether R17 could have a problem I've missed.

R17 says: "There exist \((a,b)\in C\) and a nonempty block \(G\subseteq\Gamma\) such that the answer profile at \((a,b)\) differs from its value at \((1,b_0)\), or is not determined there in the claimed way, and this contrast is lost when the components of \(G\) are deleted from \(E\)."

"This contrast is lost" — what is "this contrast"? It's the difference between the answer at (a,b) and the answer at (1,b_0), or the non-determination at (a,b).

"This contrast is lost when the components of G are deleted" — the contrast disappears when G is deleted. This means with G deleted, the answers at both points are the same (or one becomes undetermined).

But what if the contrast is "not determined at (a,b)"? Then "this contrast is lost" means... the non-determination is lost? The answer becomes determined? Or the non-determination persists but at both points?

The new wording clarifies: "evaluated at \((\tau(a),\sigma(b))\) and at \((1,\sigma(b_0))\), the answers of \(E\) with \(G\) deleted are determined and equal, or an answer that \(E\) determines at one of these points is not determined there once \(G\) is deleted."

So "the contrast is lost" means EITHER:
1. Both answers become determined and equal (the difference is gone), OR
2. An answer that was determined becomes undetermined (the determination is gone).

For the case where the original contrast was "not determined at (a,b)":
- If with G deleted, both answers are determined and equal → contrast lost (the non-determination at (a,b) becomes a determination that matches baseline). ✓
- If with G deleted, an answer that E determines becomes undetermined → contrast lost. But wait — if the original contrast was "not determined at (a,b)," then E doesn't determine the answer at (a,b). So "an answer that E determines at one of these points" would be the answer at (1,b_0) (which E determines). If with G deleted, the answer at (1,b_0) becomes undetermined, the contrast is lost (both points are now undetermined). ✓

Hmm, but this is a bit odd. If the original contrast is "not determined at (a,b)" and with G deleted the answer at (1,b_0) also becomes undetermined, is the "contrast" really lost? Both are undetermined — there's no contrast (both are the same: undetermined). So yes, the contrast is lost. ✓

But what if with G deleted, the answer at (a,b) remains undetermined and the answer at (1,b_0) remains determined? Then there's still a contrast (undetermined vs. determined). The contrast is NOT lost. The condition fails for this G.

This makes sense — the condition requires G to be necessary for the contrast. If the contrast persists after G is deleted, G is not necessary. ✓

I think R17's condition is well-defined and correct. ✓

One more thing: R17 says "evaluated at \((\tau(a),\sigma(b))\) and at \((1,\sigma(b_0))\)" — these are the translated edit-boundary pairs. But the condition "the answer profile at \((a,b)\) differs from its value at \((1,b_0)\)" is about the answer profile Ans_p(a,b) and Ans_p(1,b_0) — which are evaluated on D, not on E.

Wait, Ans_p(a,b) = Q(D, a, b) — this is the target's answer at (a,b). And the account's answer is Ans_E(τ(a), σ(b)) = Q(E, τ(a), σ(b)). The condition (A) says Ans_E(τ(a),σ(b)) = Ans_p(a,b).

R17 says "the answer profile at \((a,b)\) differs from its value at \((1,b_0)\)" — this is about Ans_p (the target's answer profile). The target's answer at (a,b) differs from the target's answer at (1,b_0).

But then R17 says "evaluated at \((\tau(a),\sigma(b))\) and at \((1,\sigma(b_0))\), the answers of \(E\) with \(G\) deleted are determined and equal" — this is about Ans_{E∖G} (the account's answer with G deleted). The account's answer at (τ(a),σ(b)) and (1,σ(b_0)) are the same (or not determined).

So the condition compares the TARGET's answers (Ans_p) for the contrast, and the ACCOUNT's answers (Ans_{E∖G}) for the deletion test. This is correct — the contrast is in the target, and the deletion test is on the account.

But there's a subtlety: the condition says "the answer profile at \((a,b)\) differs from its value at \((1,b_0)\)" — this is Ans_p(a,b) vs. Ans_p(1,b_0). And "the answers of \(E\) with \(G\) deleted are determined and equal" — this is Ans_{E∖G}(τ(a),σ(b)) vs. Ans_{E∖G}(1,σ(b_0)).

The contrast in Ans_p is lost when G is deleted from E. This means: the difference in the target's answers is no longer reflected in the account's answers (with G deleted). This is what we want — G is necessary for the account to reflect the target's contrast.

I think this is correct. ✓

But wait — the condition says "this contrast is lost when the components of \(G\) are deleted from \(E\)." "This contrast" refers to the difference in Ans_p(a,b) and Ans_p(1,b_0). But the deletion is from E (the account), not from D (the target). How can deleting from E affect the contrast in D?

The contrast in D (Ans_p) is a fact about the target. Deleting components from E doesn't change D's answer profile. So "this contrast is lost" can't mean the contrast in D is lost.

I think "this contrast is lost" means: the contrast (between the answer at (a,b) and the answer at (1,b_0)) is no longer visible in the account E∖G. With G present, the account's answers reflect the contrast (they differ). With G deleted, the account's answers don't reflect the contrast (they're the same or undetermined).

So "this contrast is lost" = "the account's ability to show the contrast is lost when G is deleted." This is a statement about E (the account), not D (the target).

Under this reading, R17's condition is: the target has a contrast (Ans_p differs at (a,b) and (1,b_0)), and the account's ability to show this contrast depends on G (deleting G loses it). This is a correct test for G's contribution.

I think R17 is correct. ✓

But the phrasing "this contrast is lost" is slightly ambiguous (it could mean the contrast in D or in E). The new wording clarifies with the colon: "this contrast is lost when the components of \(G\) are deleted from \(E\) (a deleted component imposes the full relation on its ports, Part II): evaluated at \((\tau(a),\sigma(b))\) and at \((1,\sigma(b_0))\), the answers of \(E\) with \(G\) deleted are determined and equal, or an answer that \(E\) determines at one of these points is not determined there once \(G\) is deleted."

The colon introduces the explanation: "this contrast is lost" means the answers of E∖G at the two points are the same or undetermined. This clarifies the ambiguity. ✓

One final thought: let me check R19 one more time. The change removes "(the bell does not explain the tide)." This is declared WORDING (the new wording says what the old said, in other words). But the new wording says LESS (it removes the parenthetical). Does "in other words" include saying less?

WORDING: "the new wording says what the old wording said, in other words."

If the old wording said "A (example)" and the new wording says "A," then the new wording says the main claim (A) but not the example. "In other words" usually means "equivalently" — saying the same thing differently. If the new wording drops the example, it's not saying the same thing differently.

But the example is not part of the claim — it's an illustration. The claim is "A." The new wording says "A" (without the illustration). So the new wording says the claim (A) in other words (without the illustration). But "in other words" is odd for removing an illustration.

Oh wait, one more thought about R08. Let me see if the problem can be fixed by reading Part II in a particular way.

"A component of \(E\) and a component of \(E'\) are of one kind on \(C\) when their signatures, read on \(C\) through \(\tau\) and \(\tau'\), coincide under a footprint bijection."

Actually, I want to also raise a point about R17 and the interaction with the old wording. The old wording required "an edit that removes or replaces a nonempty block of Γ while preserving the other boundary conditions." The new wording requires "a pair (a,b) and a block G such that the answer at (a,b) differs from baseline and this contrast is lost when G is deleted."

These are genuinely different conditions. Let me think about whether the change could make an account pass non-circular dependence that shouldn't.

Consider the "p because p" case: an account whose only component restates the answer. Under the old condition: the edit "remove the restatement component" removes/replaces a commitment. The answer changes (ceases to be determined). Old condition passes. But non-circular dependence's Part 1 (answer doesn't appear) fails. So the account is rejected.

Under the new condition: the edit "change the input" creates a contrast (answer changes). This contrast is lost when the restatement component is deleted. New condition passes. But Part 1 still fails. So the account is rejected.

Both reject. ✓

Consider a case where Part 1 passes but the contrast test should fail. Is there such a case?

An account where the answer doesn't appear as a component (Part 1 passes) but the commitments don't really do the work (the contrast test should fail). For example, an account with two commitments: one doing the work and one trivial. The contrast test requires SOME G whose deletion loses a contrast. G = {working commitment} would work. So the contrast test passes.

But the trivial commitment does no work. The contrast test doesn't catch this — it just requires SOME G to work. This is fine — the test is about whether there EXISTS a genuine dependence, not about whether ALL commitments do work.

Part VI handles the "does no work" case: "A commitment \(d\) does no work by itself in the candidate when every support stays a support after \(d\) is added to it and after \(d\) is removed from it." This is a separate analysis (support/criticality), not part of non-circular dependence.

So R17's condition is about the existence of a genuine dependence (some G does work). It's not about every commitment doing work. This is consistent with the theory. ✓

I don't see a case where R17's condition passes but shouldn't (given that Part 1 is separate). ✓

"In the claimed way" — the account claims a way of determining the answer (by evaluating E). "Not determined in the claimed way" means the answer is not determined by E's evaluation method. This could mean E gives no answer, or gives multiple answers, or gives an answer the account didn't claim.

## Points

**1. R08, task (c). Forward references in Part II.**

The new wording of R08 stands in Part II ("Kinds are edit-signatures"), at line 119 of the revised text. It introduces and uses the following, none of which Part II defines:

- \(E\) and \(E'\): organizations belonging to explanatory candidates (defined in Part V: "An explanatory candidate for question \(p\) is an organization \(E\), a transport \(t=(\pi,\tau,\sigma,\lambda)\) from \(D\) to \(E\)").
- \(\tau\) and \(\tau'\): the edit translations of the candidate transports (defined in Part IV: "\(\tau\) translates edits").
- \(\lambda(k)\): the anchor of a component, a subnetwork assignment (defined in Part IV: "\(\lambda\) assigns each component of \(E\) a subnetwork of \(D\) with a port translation").
- "hidden ports projected away": the projection in (F1), "\(\operatorname{proj}_{V_k}\!\big[\operatorname{Sol}_{\lambda(k)}(a,b)\big]\)" (Part V).
- "the port translation": the port-mapping component of \(\lambda\) (Part IV).

Part II currently defines organizations, roles, and kinds within a single organization. The existing sentence "Fix an organization \(D\) and a contract \(C\subseteq A\times B\) (Part III)" gives the type of \(C\) inline, so the reader is not lost. R08 gives no types for any of its new symbols. A reader at line 119 cannot determine what "read on \(C\) through \(\tau\)" does, what \(\lambda(k)\) is, or what "hidden ports projected away" means, without jumping forward to Parts IV and V and back.

The declaration is accurate as to content: the new wording does define cross-organization kinds and does state what Derivations 1 and 2 use. The defect is one of placement. The same sentences, placed at the end of Part IV or the start of Part V (where \(\tau\), \(\lambda\), and \(E\) are in scope), would be coherent. As drafted, in Part II, they are not.

*Proposed repair.* Either move the two sentences to Part V (after the transport \(t=(\pi,\tau,\sigma,\lambda)\) is defined), or add inline glosses in Part II: "…read on \(C\) through \(\tau\) and \(\tau'\), the edit translations of the transports to \(E\) and \(E'\) (Part IV)…".

**2. R15, task (a) and task (c). "Offers as doing the work" is ambiguous against Part VI and O45.**

R15 types Γ as "those the candidate offers as doing the work." The phrase is ambiguous between (i) "includes among the components presented as part of the explanatory mechanism" and (ii) "explicitly describes as doing work." Under reading (ii), the second spring in O45 — which "is never described as doing anything until the first is deleted" — would not be in Γ. The fixed verdict on O45 says the second spring "was in the account from the start." Reading (ii) would move the theory's verdict away from the fixed one.

The revised text elsewhere resolves the ambiguity in favour of reading (i). Part VI states: "A route already present in the candidate is a route whether or not anyone has described its work," and "the supports assessed are subsets of the written \(\Gamma\), whether or not anyone has set them out." A careful reader, taking these together with R15, would adopt reading (i) and reach the fixed verdict.

But the declaration itself repeats the ambiguous phrase without resolving it ("those the candidate offers as doing the work"). A reader of the declaration alone is left with the ambiguity.

*Proposed repair.* Replace "those the candidate offers as doing the work" with "those the candidate identifies as active, whether or not their work has been described."

**3. R01, task (b). "Blind" is not stated in the body.**

R01 is declared ORDER: "the new wording states, in its place, only what the text already states elsewhere." The new wording adds two elements: "blind" before "variation" and "with no represented target in that history" after.

The second element is stated in Part IV: "a selected transport has no represented target and no criticism in its history." The first element, "blind," appears nowhere in Parts I–XVI. Part IV defines selection as "a population \(\mathcal T\) of candidate transports, a variation operator \(\mu\) on \(\mathcal T\)" and says "No member of the history represents \(t\), \(H\), or the survival condition." These rule out guidance by representation and criticism, but do not say the variation operator is blind in the stronger sense of being undirected in every way (an operator could respond to environmental cues without involving a represented target or criticism).

If "blind" is read strictly (variation undirected in any way), it adds a claim beyond what the body states and the ORDER classification is wrong. If read loosely (a conventional shorthand for "not directed by representation or criticism"), it restates Part IV and the classification holds. In context — the sentence contrasts "selected" with "constructed, produced by an episode of conjecture and criticism" — the loose reading is natural. But the strict reading is available.

**4. R17, task (c). \(G\) collides with an equation tag and with another variable.**

R17 introduces \(G\) as the variable for a block of commitments. The letter is already used as: the equation tag \(\tag{G}\) for the Origin definition in Part X, and the variable \(G\subseteq K\times F\) for a purpose in Part XI's aesthetic apparatus. The task names "a letter already bound or a label shared with an equation tag" as a coherence issue to look for; both apply here. In practice the parenthesised tag and the math-mode variable are typographically distinct, but when the text refers to "condition (G)" and "block G" the reader must track the difference. A different variable name (for instance \(H'\) or \(g_0\)) would remove the clash at no cost.

## Points

### 1. R08, task (c). Seven terms undefined at line 119.

The new wording stands in Part II ("Kinds are edit-signatures"). It reads, at line 119:

> "A component of \(E\) and a component of \(E'\) are of one kind on \(C\) when their signatures, read on \(C\) through \(\tau\) and \(\tau'\), coincide under a footprint bijection; Derivation 2 uses kinds in this sense. Derivation 1 makes the like comparison between a component \(k\) of \(E\), read on \(C\) through \(\tau\), and its anchor \(\lambda(k)\), read on \(C\) directly with its hidden ports projected away, up to the port translation."

At this point in the text, the following are undefined:

- \(E, E'\): organizations of explanatory candidates, defined in Part V ("An explanatory candidate for question \(p\) is an organization \(E\), a transport \(t=(\pi,\tau,\sigma,\lambda)\) from \(D\) to \(E\)").
- \(\tau, \tau'\): edit translations of the candidate transports, defined in Part IV ("\(\tau\) translates edits").
- \(\lambda\): the anchor map, defined in Part IV ("\(\lambda\) assigns each component of \(E\) a subnetwork of \(D\) with a port translation").
- "hidden ports projected away": the projection in (F1), Part V.
- "the port translation": the port-mapping component of \(\lambda\), Part IV.

Part II currently defines organizations, roles, and kinds within a single organization. Its one existing forward reference — "Fix an organization \(D\) and a contract \(C\subseteq A\times B\) (Part III)" — gives the type of \(C\) inline. R08 provides no types at all. A reader at line 119 cannot evaluate "read on \(C\) through \(\tau\)" or identify \(\lambda(k)\) without navigating to Part IV and returning.

The declaration is accurate as to content. The defect is one of placement: the same sentences, with their symbols in scope, would be coherent in Part IV (after transports) or Part V (after candidates).

*Proposed repair.* Move the two sentences to Part V, immediately after the transport \(t=(\pi,\tau,\sigma,\lambda)\) is defined, or add inline glosses in Part II: "…through \(\tau\) and \(\tau'\), the edit translations of the transports to \(E\) and \(E'\) (Part IV)… and its anchor \(\lambda(k)\), the subnetwork of \(D\) that \(k\) is mapped to (Part IV)…".

### 2. R15, tasks (a) and (c). "Offers as doing the work" is ambiguous against Part VI and O45.

R15 types Γ (line 231) as "those the candidate offers as doing the work." The phrase admits two readings:

(i) "includes among the components presented as part of the explanatory mechanism, whether or not their work has been described"; and
(ii) "explicitly describes as contributing to the outcome."

Under reading (ii), the second spring in O45 — which "is never described as doing anything until the first is deleted" — would fail to qualify as "offered as doing the work" and would fall outside Γ. The fixed verdict says the second spring "was in the account from the start." Reading (ii) would move the verdict away from the fixed one.

The revised text resolves the ambiguity in favour of reading (i). Part VI states: "A route already present in the candidate is a route whether or not anyone has described its work," and "the supports assessed are subsets of the written \(\Gamma\), whether or not anyone has set them out." A careful reader would adopt reading (i). But R15's declaration itself repeats the ambiguous phrase ("those the candidate offers as doing the work") without resolving it, and a reader who encounters R15 before Part VI has no basis for choosing.

I checked R15's typing of the background — "including any that assigns an input" — and found it consistent with the rest of Part V: an input-assigning component is background only when it is outside Γ (the phrase modifies "the components of \(E\) outside \(\Gamma\)"), not categorically. No pull between R15 and R20 (which says the commitments of \(E|W\) are \(W\)).

*Proposed repair.* Replace "those the candidate offers as doing the work" with "those the candidate identifies as active, whether or not their work has been described."

### 3. R01, task (b). "Blind" is not stated in Parts I–XVI.

R01 is declared ORDER: the new wording "states, in its place, only what the text already states elsewhere." The new wording adds "blind" before "variation" and "with no represented target in that history" after.

The second element is stated in Part IV: "a selected transport has no represented target and no criticism in its history." The word "blind" appears nowhere in Parts I–XVI. Part IV defines selection as "a population \(\mathcal T\) of candidate transports, a variation operator \(\mu\) on \(\mathcal T\)" and constrains the history ("No member of the history represents \(t\), \(H\), or the survival condition"). These rule out guidance by representation and criticism but do not say the variation operator is blind in the strict sense of being undirected in every way (an operator could respond to environmental cues that involve no representation).

If "blind" is read strictly, it adds a claim the body does not state and the ORDER classification is wrong. If read loosely — as a conventional shorthand for "not directed by representation or criticism" — it restates Part IV and the classification holds. The sentence's contrast with "constructed, produced by an episode of conjecture and criticism" favours the loose reading. I flag the issue but conclude the change stands.

### 4. R17, task (c). \(G\) shares a letter with equation tag (G) and with the Part XI variable \(G\).

R17 introduces \(G\subseteq\Gamma\) as the block of commitments deleted in the contrast test. The letter is already the label of the Origin equation tag \(\tag{G}\) in Part X, and the variable \(G\subseteq K\times F\) (a purpose) in Part XI's aesthetic apparatus. The task names "a letter already bound or a label shared with an equation tag" as a coherence issue; both apply. In practice the parenthesised tag and the math-mode variable are typographically distinct, and no passage in the text conflates them, but the reuse of the letter across three roles invites unnecessary tracking. Renaming the block to, say, \(H_0\) or \(g\) would remove the clash at no cost.

---

R01: STANDS
R08: FALLS — seven terms used before defined in Part II; forward references cannot carry the definition
R15: STANDS
R17: STANDS

OVERALL: NEEDS REPAIR

END OF REPORT

R08's forward references DON'T have inline types. E, E', τ, τ', λ are used without any type information. This IS a coherence failure.

Part II defines organizations (line ~100: \(D=(V,(X_v)_{v\in V},J,B,A,L)\)), roles, and kinds within one organization. The following symbols and operations appear in the new wording without definition in Part II:

- **"the port translation"** is the port-mapping component of \(\lambda\), Part IV.

### 2. R15, tasks (a) and (c). "Offers as doing the work" is ambiguous against Part VI and O45.

R15 at line 231 types the commitments:
